PRINCE_TO THRONE — 


Paris, Feb. 6. 


A remarkable story of a Royalist plot in Germany is 
told by the Basle Correspondent of Le Temps, who states 
that he received his story from people who left Berlin. 


-The correspondent states that the crisis .in Berlin 
was brought to a head by Herr Himmler, ‘Chief of | the 
Secret Police, who, on January 30, handed Herr Hitler a 
dossier purporting to show that General von Fritsch and 
other generals had formed a_ secret. organisation for 


putting the second son of the ex-Crown Prince on the 


throne. ; 

The correspondent's informants 
referred to the well-known animosity 
between Herr Himmler sand’ the 
Reichswehr, ‘and asserted that 
Himmler last summer gave the Sovict 
Ambassador information, with regard 
to seeret contacts between certain 
German -generals and, the Soviot 
General Staff, which resulted in the 
execution of Marshal Tukachevsky 
and others, 

There were rumours in Berlin of 
the Imminent arrest by the police of 
certaln generals, whereupon, It “Is 
statod, General von Fritsch called: a 


-{seeret meeting and on the morning 


of February 28 sent o company of 
regular troops to occupy a house on 
the Wilhelmstrasse, near the ‘Chan- 
evllery, ‘This was done, the report 
states, In order to show Herr Himmler 


that von Fritsch was ready to meet’ 


force with’ force, 


Various strategic measures were 
olso taken in different points In 
Berlin, after which Herr Keltel began 
negotiations with Horr Hitler on be- 
half of the Army.—Reuter, —

AMERICA 
WILLING 


=|TO DISARM || 


Cordell Hull Appeals 


Washington, Feb. 6. 

The willingness of the United 
-States—Government—to—limit—or. 
reduce armaments was ex- 
pressed by Mr, Cordell Hull, the 
Secretary of State, in. broad- 
cast over a nation-wide network 
to-day. : 


“While, in a world in which Inq 
creased construction of armaments Is 
a regrettable fact, we sre compelled 
to render adequate our military and 
naval establishments, wo are ready 
ut any time to Join other nations in a 
common effort to bring about a 
general limitation (‘or reduction of 
armaments,” Mr, Hull ‘declared. 


The Secretary of State. nttacked 
what he termed “hermit’ nations,” 
‘meaning natlons which were secking 
economic self-sufficiency, 


He declared that economle warfare 


Lwar lurgely responsible for the re- 


cent alarming disintegration of inter- 
national’ relationships, ond war, and 
the fear‘ of war, were gripping the 
minds and stultifying the spirit of 
mankind. 


Feverish preparations for potential 
armed conflicts were sapping the 
already badly Impalred economic 
health of many nations, 


“The world desperately needs in- 
ternational order based on the re- 
vitatisation of international law,” Mr, 
Hull declared, 


He concluded -his broadcast by: 
offerlng as onc means of peace his 
programme of trade agreements. — 
Reuter, e

F ‘ Cupe Town. 

A cloudburst, which flooded — the 
basement of n holst room at the Wel- 
xedacht Mine, Johannesburg, resulted 
In 100 miners being marooned under- 
ground, European workers were 
stranded for 18 hours and natives for 
21 hours, The machinery was put 
out of action. 


Raln turned the surrounding coun- 
tryside into a lake, the torrent filling 
ihe basement of the holst room to 0 
depth of ft. : 


The fire brigade was six hours 
pumping out the water, and the 
stranded men were brought up thls 
afternoon after an nll-night wait in 
‘the mine.

Chun us Chairmin of Szechuen Pro-

power, while most merchants and

Ching as a nominee of the Central

Forts Attacked 


* Canton, Feb. 6. 

Bocca Tigris forts were twice as- 
sanulted yesterday by four warships 
supported by 13 planes. After the 
engagement had been going on (Cor 
seme 40 minutes from 6 u.m., one of 
the ships, says the Tai Chung news 
agency, was hit and caught fire. 
The planes immediately put up a 
smoke-sereen to enable the vessel to 
get away. By 8 a.m. the battle was 
over, with the Japanese ships, retir- 
ing, but an hour later cight more ships 
returned with 20 planes. ~So thick 
and fast did the shells from the anti- 
aircraft guns explode in the midst, 
of the cir fleet that only 18 bombs 
were dropped. 

Unquestionably, the ficct was bent 
on running pust the forts, but the 
order given to turn all the fort guns 
that were in range on the leader 
piaced it Into'a position that it could 
neither stem = ahead or draw back. 
Only a smoke-sereen created by the 
plines enabled it to get out of the 
danger zone. By the time the screen 
ralsed, It war scen that the whole 
flect was withdrawing and ‘this 
second battle Jasted an hour—

To Be Spurned . 


Washington, Feb, 6. 

Anticipating that Japan will spurn 
the recent three-power ultimatum, 
it is learned that the Navy Depart- 
ment is preparing designs for the 
construction of 40,000 and 46,000 ton 
dreadnoughts, . 

_The Department hos exhaustively 
tested model hulls In the model ship 
basin to determine the most suitable 
design for the size of construction. 

OMiclals hinted that ‘there might be 
some radical new features, but they 
do not expect a definite snnounce- 
ment of plans until? after February

i Washington, Feb. 6.

ment expenditures instead of

tened to place an obstacle in the 
way of the. Navy programme.

“BOILER 
EXPLODES 


Eleven Injured In 
Mongkok Blast 


Ten men and a woman were In- 
jured in Mongkok about 10 a.tn. 
yesterday when a steam boiler, made 
.from what appeared to be a large 
ol! contalner, exploded In the kitchen 
of 65, Tong MI Street, second floor, 
one of three flats occupled by the 
Wal Kee Cheong yarn manufactory. 


Four of the Injured are now in the 
Kowloon Hospltal.and they are: Lam 
Fook-cheong, 43; Sul Yeung, 25; 
Cheung Kal-ting, 19; and Chung 
Sze-mui, 30-year old) woman. The 


firat three are suffering fram senlds 
while the woman has injurics to the 
forehead and head. The temaining 
seven received superficial Injuries, 
and after having been treated In the 
hospital were allowed to lenve. 


Believed to have been In the kitchen 
in charge of the steam gauge at the 
time, the mon Lam {s the most 
seriously hurt. All the injured were 
employees of the. manufactory, with 
the exception of the woman, who 
was in the backyard on the foor 
below when a shower of bricks, 
caused by the explosion, fell on’ her. 

About 13 persons were working in 
the flat at the time of ‘the ‘binst. 
The boiler was In the kitchen, and 
it was learned from o* man working 
there that it was used to produce 
steam to dry the yarn whlle it was 
being wound on spools. 


Immediately after the explosion, 
the police and the Fire Brigade were 
summoned, and the injured, the maj- 
ority of whom suffered cuts through 
being struck by the bricks, were 
taken to hospital, . 


The cause of the explosion is being 
investigated by the . pollee under 
Detectlve-Inspector A. E, Carey.

INKING NOW

: London, Feb. 5. 

The Admiralty have received con- 
firmation of the sinking of the 
British 1,837-ton steamer Alcira 
curly yesterday morning, five miles 
east of Barcelona, by aircraft. All 
the crew of 25 were rescued.

which was carrying 1,800 tons of 
coal, in addition to flying the British 
flag and International Control flag, 
had a Union Jaek painted. prominent-

board was an observation officer, 
under the non-Intervention control 
scheme, of Britlsh natlonality, who 
Was ulso rescued.—British Wireless,

The true sittation, judging by neu- 
tral reports, Is that while ‘Ttalian 
forces are still in possession -of all 
Ethiopian provincial capitals, the 
country Is far from quiet, and politi- 
cally and economically has yet to be 
an asset to Italy. Guerilla bands 
are actively harassing Italian scttle- 
ments and thelr sorties on the rall- 
ways are-stated to make travel un- 
safe, except in the areas immediate- 
ly surrounding the cilies with Italian 
tarrisons, 


A few phases of Italian activities 
affording a good Idea.of the gencral 
situation are depicted in an Italian 
military journal which by no means 
can be considered to be overpainting 
the picture. At La Libela, 200 miles 
north of Addis Ababa, 400 Italian 
troops, it says, were receritly com- 
pletely surrounded by Ethioplans, 
while a relief column of 200 men 
met a similar fate at Bilbala Gorges. 
The Italians were saved’ only by 
large scale aerial operations in which 
113 machines from Diredawa drop- 
ped food and munitions to them, .as 
well os bombing and machine gun- 
ning the Ethiopians, who were final- 
ly routed.

more likely this haz been caused by

~ Move Simultaneously 


The British Embassy late 


Tokyo, Feb. 5. 


this afternoon delivered a note to 


the Foreign Office which is reported to request Japan to adhere 


to the main clauses of the 


1936 Naval Treaty. 


— It fs understood that the United States [embassy has re- 
-eelved similar instructions, but the Forcign OMce declares that 
the American note has not yet been delivered —Retter, 


CONTENTS OF NOTE-~ 


: London, Feb. Bs 
Following Is the text of the note 

communicated by the Britlsh Am- 

bnssador, Sir Robert Craigie: 

“The Japanese Government will be 
aware that under the London Naval 
Trealy of 1036 and corresponding 
bilateral agreements with Germuny 
and - Soviet Russla, His Majesty's 
Government in the United Kingdom 
are precluded from constructing. 
enplital ships—that is vessels of more 
than 10,000 tons standard displace- 
ment or with a fun of more than 5 
inches—which exceed 35,000 tons or 
carry a gun of more than 10 inchcs 
or which sre less than 17,500 tons or 
carry a gun of less than 10 inches, 

“As regards crulsers—that {3 
vessels of not more thon 10,000 tons 
with n sun of not more than 8 inches 
—Ilis' Majesty's Government are 
ilmited to a maximum of 8,000 tons 
with 6 Inch guns. : 

“The Japanese Government have 
unfortunately not seen thelr way to 
subscribing to the London - Naval 
Treaty nor have they hitherts felt 
nble to give any assurances that 
treaty mits would In practice be 
adhered to by them, As the Japan- 
ese Government will be aware, the 
naval treaties give His Majesty's 
Government the right of escalation 
in ‘the event of bullding not in con- 
formity with treaty limits by a 
Power not party thereto. 

PERSISTENT REPORTS 

“There have for some time been 
persistent and cumulative reports, 
which: In the absence of explicit’ as- 
suranees from the’ Jupanese Govern- 
ment that they are ill-founded must 
be deemed to be. authentic, that 
Japan has undertaken or intends to 
undertake construction. of capital 
ships and cruisers not in conformity 
with the above mentioned limit, 

“His Majesty's Government have 
therefore decided that it will be 
necessary for them to exercise their 
right of escalation, unless the Japan- 
ese Government can furnish the 
aforesald assurances and can satisfy 
His Majesty's Government that they 
will not prior to January 1, 1943, lay 
down, complete or acquire any 
vessel which docs not conform to the 


Aimits. In question without previously 


informlny, His Majesty's Government 
of their intention to do so and of the 
tonnayte. and calibre of the largest 
yun of the vessel or vessels concern- 


THE NAVAL ESTIMATES 


“In view. of the ‘forthcoming 
publication of naval estimates and 
-the- necessity forgiving other Treaty. 
Powers information os_ to intended 
British construction, His Majesty's 
Government ‘will be glad to receive 
a reply not later than February 20 
next. Should no reply be received 
by that date.or should the reply be 


“tion with other naval 


lacking In the desired information 
and assurances, they will be com- 
pelled to assume that the Japanese 
Government either are constructing, 
or acquiring, or have authorised con- 
struction or acquisition of, vessels 
not In conformity: with the lUmils 
referred to. 

“His Majesty's Government swould 
thereupon be obliged, in consulta~ 
Powers with 
whom they nre in treaty relations, to 
resume ‘full HMberty of action. If, 
however the Japanese Government, 
though engaged in or intending to 
engage In construction not -In |.con- 
formity ' with treaty limits, . were 
willing to indicate forthwith tonnarcs 
and calibres of guns of vessels which 
they were constructing or were in- 
tending to construct, His Majesty's 
Government for thelr part would be 
rcady to discuss with the Japanese 
Government the question of tonnages 
and gun callbres to be odhered to 
In the future, If Japno were now 
prepared to agree to some limitation. 
It would, however, be necessary that

New York To London In Twelve 
Hours Is Forecast 
By Airline Officials — 


By JOHN C, BRUNJES 
United Press Staff Correspondent 


: New York, Jan. 30. 
TWELVE-HOUR, non-stop flight from New York to London in 
giant, luxurious airplanes carrying 100 or moro passengers, will 

be possible as carly as 1941, according to announcements made by 

officials of the Pan-American Airways. , 

Airmail operations across the Atlantic by the summer of. 
1938 is now virtually a certainty. Pan-American Airways, Im- 
perial Airways of Great Britain, and Deutsche Lufthansa of Ger- 
many, have all completed flight. survey programmes and signified 
their intention of conducting scheduled flights across the North 
-Atlantie-during-1938,—Passenger-service,-however,—will -not-begin- 


into service, 


payload of 25,000 pounds. The 
cruising speed of 233 miles per

liners will rival that of the most 
modern steamships. There will 
be. private stateroom accom. 
modation for 100 passengers, 
private dressing rooms, a socinl 
and dining lounge, a_ galley 
having adequate facilities for 
the preparation and storage of 
food, baths, hot and - cold run- 
ning water-and many other con- 
veniences. There will ‘also ‘be 
accommodations for a crew 
i sixteen, 


In addition, the giant ships will be 
entirely alr-conditioncd and sound- 
proofed. A constant atmospheric pres- 
sure will be maintained so that as- 
cent and descent of the plane will be 
unnoticeable, 


Elght prominent United States air- 
plane manufacturers were — recently 
invited by Pan-American to submit 
plans and bids for the construction 
of a “revolutionary” type of alrcratt 
to be used In trans-oceanic tying 
The only requirement specified by 
the airline was that the design must 
be no safe aircraft practical to operate, 


Two manufacturers, Igor Slkorsky,

Division of United Aircraft Corpora-

of the Bocing Aircraft Corporation, 
were of the opinion that, despite the 
strict requirements Inid down by the 
airline, development of such alreraft 
was entirely possible,

MIDDLESEX IN LEAD 


IDDLESEX assumed = Jeadership 

of the First Division by taking 
both points from” Eastern. The 
soldicrs settled down more quickly 
than the Chinese, and because of 
this they were able to score two foals 
jn quick: succession in the first half. 
These {wo reverses scemed to take 
the heart out of the Eastern players, 
who were not the same tenm they 
have been In recent weeks. The ‘ 
success of the Middlesex ean be 
traced to the fine work of the half- 
backs, Wilkinson, Bright and Fresh- 
water, who held the usually nippy 
Eastern forwards In a vicce-lilse grip. 
Though one point ahead of South 
China “B,” the Middlesex have play- 
ed three matches more than the Caro- 
line Hill side. 


K. CHINESE IMPROVE 


O. 13 may be unlucky for some 

people, but it certalnly has proved 
the turning point for the Kowloon 
Chinese. Up to the time they held 
the Senforths to a draw, they had not 
won a single point from 12 matches. 
But in thelr thirteenth match, they 
played: the Scaforths to a draw, 
and following up this performance, 
they met ond defeated the Police at 
-Caroline -Hill_on_ Saturday -_by_the. 
odd stoal in five. True, thelr victory 
‘was due more to the poor form shown 
by the guardians of the peace than to 
thelr own good play; but nevertheless 
it speaks well for their opportunism 
that they were able to take avantage 
of it, I hope this will mark the turn- 
ing of the tlde. There are several 
good players among the Kowloon 
Chinese, but Inck of confidence often 
prevents them from giving of their 
best. 


‘FOWLER'S FOUR GOALS 


NOTHER team to stage a resuscit- | 
tlon an Saturday was the Club 
who, after a long series of defeats, 
came back to beat the Seaforths, It 
was a fine performance, but chief 
credit for this must go to F. Fowler. 
Without much assistance from his 
colleagues in the forward Mne, 
Freddie broke through In fine style on 
scverul occassions, and scored four 
goals on hls own to give the Club 
victory by 4-3. His bustling type 
of game completely disorganised the 
Seaforth defence. The Club's suc- 
ecss was all the more surprising in. 
view of the fact that many of those 
who turn out were junior players,

AN OLD FAVOURITE. 


Amongst those who watched the 
trial was Talbot, the Royal Welch 
Fusiliers’ inside forward, who re 
turned to the Colony. with. his batta- 
lion from Shanghai «on, Saturday. 
He will be leaving this week-end, 
but few know that with a little better 
luck we might have had him with us 
as ho was desirous’ of joining’ the 
Police Foree, This is not to be, 
however, and the Colony thus Joses 
another fine footballer.

MANILA BOXING: 


Boy Wonder Champion 
Of The Orient 


Manila, Feb, 5. 
Dispelling all doubt os to his 
claims to be the Welterwelght Cham- 
plon of the Orient, Andra Shelloeff, 
the Harbin and Shanghal “Boy Won-- 
der”, lust night knocked out “Clever” 
Henry, the Filipino, from whom he 
captured the title a fortnight ago, in 
the second round of caciy scheduled 
ten-round fight. The Russian had 
won by a knock-out In the third 
round at their first meeting, the 
FiJipino then saying that he was nbt 
In first-class condition. 


The return fight, which took place 
last night, was witnessed by a capa- 
city crowd numbering ten thousand, 
and proved once and for all that the 
new title holder Is the better man. 

The Russian started in whirlwind 
fashion, und before the spectators had 
settled down in thelr sents floored 
the Filipino, who rose after a count 
of five. The knock-down occurred 
in the first minute of the fight. 


With prim determination written 
all over his face, the Russlan weighed 
Into his opponent at the start of the 
second round. He shot over three 
jubs in the mid-section of Henry, 
and then followed up with devastat- 
ing right to the jaw which spelt finis 
for the Filipino, who was still dead 
to the world when the count was 
over, ‘ 

Shelact weighed in at 14314 Ibs., 


| and the Filipino at 143 Ibs,—Reuter,

“Boy Wonder,” who Jast month cap- 
tured the welterweight title of the 
Orient, and “Clever” Henry, the 

EiMpino loser, 3 


The winner of to-night’s bout will 
be undisputed welterwelght king of 
the Orient. ‘

Hockey Visit To 
Macao By C.B.A. 


The Central British Associa- 
tion hockey team visited Macao 
yesterday and played a drawn 
match with the Portuguese 
colony, each side scoring once. 

An account of the match 
will be published in the second 
edition to-day. 


EXHIBITION HOCKEY 


Civilians Beaten By ‘Air 
Force And Army 


Playing on the Hockey. Club 
xround yesterday, 9 strong Army and 
Air Foree hockey team beat a team 
of Civilians by three goals to onc. 
‘With a forward line composed of 
sharp shooters who combincd : well, 
the Services found the net twice 
through Pritam Nath and ‘Sawal 
Khan, G. Singh reducing the lead 
just before the interval. 

Plny continucd from one end of 
the field to the other during the, 
second half, both sides working 
hard to score, the exchanges being 
slightly In favour’ of the Services. 
From a scramble near the goal, 
Sawal Khan netted with a fine shot 
about ten minutes before time.: 

For the winners, Gulam Rasul at 
left-back -played -a_fine_ spoiling. and_| 
defensive game; while the Civillan 
left back Hayward, and Brown, right 

half, defended well for the losers. . 


TENNIS SERIES © 


Perth, Feb. 5. 

The German and Australian lawn

There is so much false pride in

Is it‘ right that a man who plays

SHEEP AND GOATS 


URELY the times have 
changed since the days 
when we hod men with £2,000 o 
year or more and plenty of leisure 
to play our national games and play 
them well. “ 
Yet our attitude to cricket has not 
altered and to retain. a false social 
status a man will go to any lengths 
to camouflage his real position. 
Cricket to-day is full of men who 
hold-decent-jobs—because._ they_ are. 
cricketers, There is such a hair- 
linc of difference between these men 
and those who are paid openly by 
the coufities to play cricket that I 
for one cannot sec It. : 
The old segregation of amateurs 


and professionals in pavillons is not} 


yet dend—anyliow at Lord’s. 
does the M.C.C. (calling itself 
this case the Board of Control): at- 
tempt ‘to keep an England team 
together when it plays Test matches 
in this country. : 

On the Inst ocession that the 
South Africans were here I person- 
ally found at Leeds that some pro- 
fessionnls were at one hotel, others 
nt another, others still (quite natur- 
ally) were at thelr homes. 

The amateurs were quartered else- 
where: the captaln was at Harrogate 
and the three selectors In three dif- 
ferent places. 

If this state of affairs exists when 
the Australians are here next season 
It will not be a very great help to 
England's team spirit. 

The Australians treat this matter 
the greatest common _ sense. 
They pay every man who comics over 
here for the loss of time he suffers; 
clerks, school-teachers, farm work- 
ers, are al! paid £000, and they live 
together as one family with nothing 
to emphasise any difference tn their 
social status, 

A former Englund captain said to 
me the other day, “L cannot see 
where the next England captain is 
coming from.” Hammond's decision 
Is the answer. 

I hope the Test sclectors will act 
with vision, ;

ADVICE TO WIVES 


BY SU 


The suggestion that birt 


RGEON 


h control should be practised 


by young wives for the first year of marriage was made by 
Mr.'L. C. Rivett, obstetric surgeon to inpatients at Queen 
Charlotte’s Hospital, when lecturing at the winter school 
for Health Visitors and School Nurses, in London. 


“It seems & me,” declared Mr. Rivett, “that it would be very 
much wiser if in the course of the development of education we 


could arrange things so that a woman does not .embark 


upon 


pregnancy until she ‘has completely settled down to her new life 
and until she has learned how to manage her own home.

terraln following a torrential rain, o

wving of the Japanese troops at Wuhu 
yesterday morning. :

counter-attack was launched by the 
Chinese forces at Lukang, seven miles 
west of Wuhu on the river. Taken 
unowares, the Japanese at “Lukang 
were driven from the strects and re- 
treated toward: the hills behind the 
town. -

vaders from Kweltan. At Chnosze- 
kang, nine miles south of Wuhu, 


harassing Japanese positions pending

1938 PROGRAMME DEPENDS 

UPON JAPAN’S ANSWER 

. : London, Feb. a. a. 

‘Tha Daily Telegraph's naval correspondent, Mr. 
‘Hector C. Bywater, says to-day that the British Navy’s 
estimates to be introduced at the end of the month will 
provide for additional battleships and cruisers, designed 
within Treaty limits—35,000 tons for the former and 8,000 
tons for the, latter. ee : 


But alternative plans exist for larger and more 


powerful types and these will be adopted if the Japanese | 


‘ reply to His Majesty’s Government’s query in respect of 

Japanese tonnage building is in any way unsatisfactory. 

It is understood it is proposed to lay down from three 

to five battleships during the coming summer and 
autumn.

Smallpox 
Since Jan. 1 


Nineteen cases of small-pox 
notified to the Hongkong health 
authorities during the week-end 
bring the total since February 
1 to 126, and the total since the 
beginning of the year to 347. 
Over 200 deaths have been re-

Japanese aircraft and warships have completely 
destroyed the modern town of Heung Chau in the Tong-

River Delta. ; : _ :

ships in the Chung Shan district and along its‘coast and 
rumours are widespread of the havoc wrought there:” 


The destruction of Heung Chau was completed by o 


heavy barrage from. the sea during the past few days.’ 


‘Casualties are not known but it is believed there were a 
number killed and many injured. :

Once the foundation recipe 
mastered, sauce making Is easy. 
A good sauce should be:—(1) Free 
from lumps, (2) of u coating consis- 
tency, (3) well cooked, (4) correctly 
seasoned and Anvoured, 
Here is the recipe for the founda- 
* tion sauce—sultably flavoured, — it 
can be served with fish, bolled meat, 
fowl, veretubles, &e.

ounce of butter or murgarine, and a 
-little pepper and salt to half a pint 
of liquid. The Hquld may be all 
mili, half milk and halt water or 
half mille and half stock (fish ment, 
é&c.) :

Add butter and seasoning ond utir 
“until the sauce bolle and thickens, |

Deating with “Stleky"” Cards 


When playing cards. have been 
much used they sometimes become 
“sticky” and unpleasant to handle, 
To make them as fresh og when new 
set them out aingly on a table and 
dust over. with oa talcum . powder, 
Both sides must be trented. Now 
collect the carda and. shuffte them 
for...a-fow minutes. . Tho powder 
falla. away, leaving the cards fresh 
and smooth to the touch. ie

ingroom with a howling gale

Pole atmosphere is not to be de- 
sired,

can be used for this .purpose.)

ing depends upon the food and 
drinks, so do go to a Jot of trou- 
ble of your own powers, -hire a 
catering firm to provide every- 
thing for you. Lemon _ and 
orange juice, ice-cream (and 
plenty of it!) as well as wine, 
sherry, and port must be on the 
menu.

have dozens of ‘smal! sausage

munges, “Individual” — fruit 
dishes, served up in grape-fruit 
glasses, are also an excelent 
idea.

bon-bons, and sweetmeats .can 
be placed in odd corners. 

The supper interval should ‘be 
quite carly in the evening, og 
this is an admirable opportunity 
for : the guests to become ac. 
quainted with one another. 

And remember a small but 
important point—ash trays! 

Follow these simple tips and 
you need have no qualms as to 
‘whether your dance is really go- 
ing to be a success.

the mine,

BOILER QUESTION 


The Editor, 
Hongkong Telegraph, . 


Sir,-The bursting of a steam boiler 
made from a large oll container 
brings us back to the need for 
regulations governing factory boilers. 
Wf I remember correctly, factory 
boilers in’ Hongkong are not subject 
to any periodic inspections, and 
there. are cases where condemned 
marine boilers are put ashore for 
further use. What would happen if 
one of these large boilers should 
blow up? 
Sxy Hic.

THE HONGKONG TELEGRAPH. MONDAY, 


CANTON 
DECLARES 
LOYALTY 


Japanese Agents 
Spreading Rumours 
Of Disaffection 


Canton, February 7. 

“All the military and civilian offi- 
clnis of the entire province — of 
Kwangtung as well as the thirty-five 
million people ure solidly behind the 
Natlonal Government and continue to 
support and unswervingly obcy the 
orders of Generalissimo Chiang Kat- 
shek," declared General Wu Teh- 
chen, Governor of Kwangtung, at the 
weekly memortal service held at the 
auditorium of the’ Kwangtung Pro- 
vincial Government carly this morn- 
ing, Continuing, the Kwangtung 
Governor emphatically declared that 
the people of his province are de- 
termined to resist the enemy to the 
bitter end. i . : 

Gencral Wu Te-chen pointed out 
that the .muny pernicious rumours 
prevailing, especlally in Hongkong, 
regarding a state of affairs in Cunton 
which does not tctually exist, are 
undoubtedly of Japanese orlgin. The 
netivities of the Japanese and their 
agents are the worst form of calumny 
imaginable as the Intention behind 
these tactics is to crente panic among 
the population and couse a misunder- 
standing, he said. : 

“We ure sure of ultimate victory. 
The fact that. absolute culmness is 
shown by all walks of life in Canton 
in spite of the wild rumours that 
have been circulated during the past 
few days ts definite proof of the 
futility of Japanese , efforts, The 
Japanese are resorting to every con- 
ccivable tricic in the hope of realizing 
their avowed policy “to divide and 
rule” China, but they can never suc- 
eced In thelr Intrigues in’ Kwangtung 
us this province is the birthplace of 
the revolution and the people here 
have always been intensely patrio- 
tle," General Wu concluded.—

Californians 
Prepare For 
Great Storms 


Snowfalls May Break 
20-year Record: 


San Francisco, Feb. 6. 

The Weather Bureau reports that 
a new storm js approaching the Call- 
fornian const from the Pacific and 
forecasts additional rain and snow. 

Snow has_ blocked virtually all 
northern Californian mountain passes 
and is expected to exceed the record 
of 277 inches In 1913, 

Storm warnings huve been issued 
practically everywhere along ‘the 
coast, and high seas are already 
delaying? shipping. 

The Sacramento River at Knight's 
Landing has reached the 30-foot stage, 
ind_is_within_two_feet_of overflow= 
ing. . 

Railways report that, at several 
places along the Unes to the interior 
30-foot drifts have been encountercd. 
—United Presa,

PARIS HEARS OF PLOT 


Insurgents 
Advance In 
Teruel Area 


Smashing Attack On 
25-Mile Front 


Saragossa, Feb, 6. 

General Franco's forces struc 
again in the Teruel sector to- 
day, and claim to have smashed 
through the Government : lincs 
cover a twenty-five mile front. 

Practically all of the territory taken 
by the Insurgents hay been held by 
the Government forces since the oul+ 
brenk of the civil war. : 

General Davila, the Insurgent 
Minister for National Defence, per- 
sonally directed the attack by three 
columns of troops, which advanced 
eastward = towards the | Alfambra 
Iver from: a Une oranning — north 
of Celadas- and parallel with the 
Soragossa road. The columns con- 
verted ‘on the outskirts of the town 
of Alfambra, the Government's local 
hendquarters, nnd swept the Govern- 
ment troops from the — helghts of 
Palomera, They renched — the road 
along Alfambra Valley, removing te 
Government thrent to the Snrayossn 
road, one of the vital Insurgent means 
of communication, . 

The clty of Terucl has ‘not yet 
been Involved In the battle. —Reuter, 

‘ PANCRUDO CAPTURED 

. Barcelona, Feb, 6. 

An oficial communique admits that 
the Insurgent forces have captured 
the villare oof Panerudo on_ the 
Teruel front, : 

The communique claims that the 
Government forces captured a hill 
in the Muelade-Teruel — sector.—

PRINCE TO THRONE 


‘ Paris, Feb.6.0 = 


.  Aremarkable story of a Royalist-plot in Germany is 
told by the Basle Correspondent of Le Temps, who states 
that he received his story from people who left Berlin- 


yesterday. : 


The correspondent states that the crisis in Berlin 
was brought to a head by Herr Himmler, ‘Chief of the 
Seeret Police, who, on January 30, handed Herr Hitler a 
dossier purporting to show that General von Fritsch and 
other generals had formed a seceret organisation for 
putting the second.son of the ex-Crown Prince on the 


throne. . ae

referred to the well-known animosity 
between Herr Himmler ond the 
Relchswehe, ond asserted that 
1mmiler last summer gave the Soviet 
Ambassador Informutlon, with regard 
to seerect contacty between certain 
German generals and the | Soviet

execution of Marshal ‘Tubsachevsky 
and others, -

shippers and their victims? ” 

It fs possible to speculate on,

many and Italy. will be like 
without Hitler and Mussolini, 
but the case of Poland can help 
us to peer a Little into the 
future. 

In that country a “leader” has 
come and gone, and In his passing 
has bequeathed thie lesson that the 
aftermath of dictatorship fs dis- 
ruption, that “leader worship " 1s 
a filmsy foundation on which to 
erect a national structure, that 
the bullding Itself, while flamboy- 
ant, Is safe only during the Ilfetime 
of the architect, and becomes a 
source of danger to adjoining 
propertics. ' 

The precedent of Poland ts in 
all respects admirable as a basls 
for judgment. Here was a new 
State, long under the heel of a 
foreign tyrant, the Russian Tsar, 
her people pllable in their pride of 
new-won independence. 

The Polish dictator was the least 
objectionable of the species, Josef 
Pilsudski, the “Old © Marshal,” 
almost single-handed,’ snatched 
Polish Independence out of the 
fires of the last war, 


E was a thundering old 

Hiwastioicrier with ao 

«record ‘of  brigandage 

and derring-do and a taste for 

plain specch thot would have 
made Rabelais blush.

_ and exploitation and compromise.”

has on ‘more than one occasion

quest, and has shown her support 
of Japan In her campalen anainst 
China.

from voting on the League resolu- 
ton. :

THE HONGKONG . TELEGRAPH. 


Chinese Guerillas Continue Grim Warfare | 


ee 


SET RING OF FIRE TO 


DRIVE JAPANESE FROM 
HILL-TOP POSITIONS © 


Kwangsi Troops Praised For 
Gallant Defence of Lines 


Nanchang, Feb. 6. 

The semi-official publication, Ww Han Jih,Pao states 
that Chinese mobile units have surrounded. the hills in 
the vicinity of Hsuenchen, south of Nanking and west of 
Kwangteh, and: set fires which are slowly creeping 
towards the summits, where Japanese troops are station- 
ed. The Japanese positions are in ‘turmoil. 

Japanese defending Hsuenchen used light field pieces 
to shoot from the tower's of the gates in the low city

IN SOCCER TRIAL GAME 


AROUND THE GROUNDS 


INTERESTING SOCCER GAMES 


OVER THE 


WEEK-END 


(By “Abo") 


Plenty of fine socecr was played over the weok-cnd. ‘Apart 
from a programme of three League matches in the First Division, 
there was a Shield re-play on Saturday, and the trial tn pre- 


paration for the 


visit of the Islington Corinthians was played 


on the Club ground yesterday. ° : 


SHIELD RE-PLAY 


j ITHOUT the services of some of 

those who turned out in the first 
match, It was not surprising that St. 
Joseph’s -could not ‘hold’ their ‘own 
atalnst Kowloon jn the re-play. 
Whereas the forward Hne was the 
weakest department in the first meet- 
Ing, this was the Saints strongest on 
Saturday. The defence however, 
failed against the Kowloon attack, 
which was strengthened by the ine 


clusion of Evans as inside-right, The . 


Kowloon players deserved their vic- 
tory; they were, on the whole, the 
better side, This encounter marked 
the last appearances of three players 
for Kowloon. Evans, Rowlands and 
Connor will be Ieaving the Colony 
this week-end. Their departure will 
make’ not only Kowloon's soccer the 
poorer, but the Colony's as well, I 
am sure all local enthusiasts will Join 
me in wishing them the best of luck 
wherever they go. 


_ MIDDLESEX IN LEAD 


IDDLESEX assumed leadership 

of the First Divislon. by taking 
both points from Eastern, The 
soldiers settled down more quicidy 
than the Chinese, and because of 
this they were able to score two goals 
in quick succession in the first half. 
These two reverses seemed to take 
the heart out of the Eastern players, 
who were not the same team they 
have been in recent weeks, The 
suc of the Middlesex can be 
traced’ to the fine work of the half- 
backs, Wilkinson, Bright and Fresh- 
water, who held the usually nippy 
Eastern forwards tn a vice-like .grip. 
Thouxh ‘one point nhead of South 
China “B," the Middlesex have pliuy- 
ed three matches more than the Caro~ 
Hine Hilt side. 


K. CHINESE IMPROVE 


O. 13 may ke unlucky for some 

people, but it certainly has proved 
the turning point for the Kowloon 
Chinese. Up to the time they held 
the Seaforths to a draw, they had not 
Won a single point from 12 matches, 
But In their thirteenth match, they 
pluyed the Seuforths to a draw, 
and following up this performance, 
they met and defeated the Police at 
Caroline Hi Saturda t 
True, th 
wus duc more to the poor form shown 
by the guardians of the peace than to 
thelr own yood play; but nevertheless 
it speaks well for their opportunism 
{hat they were able to take avantage 
of it. IT hope this will mark the turn- 
ing of the tide. There are several 
good players among the Kowloon 
Chinese, but lack of confidence often 
prevents them from giving of their 
Cnt. 


FOWLER'S FOUR GOALS 


NOTHER team to stage a resuscit~ 
tion on Saturday was the Club 
who, after a long series of defeats, 
came back to beat the Seaforths, It 
was a fine performance, but ‘chief 
credit for thls must go to F. Fowler, 
Without much assistance from his 
colleagues In the forward 
Freddie broke through in fine style on 
several occassions,-and scored four 
foals on his own to give the Club 
victory by 4-3, His bustling type 
of name completely disorganised the 
Seaforth defence. The Club's suc- 
cess was all the more surprising in 
view of the fact that many of those 
who turned out were Junior players, 
YESTERDAY'S ‘TRIAL 

ue trial match played yesterday 
is described In another column, 
and there fs no need for-me to say 
much here. What struck spectators 
most forclbly was.the weakness of 
the Associatlon’s attack, which suf- 
fered in comparison with that of the 
Chinese, There ore not many food 
eentre-forwards-in the Colony at the 
moment, Personally I would plumb 
for Chan Tok-fal, the South China 
Teadcr, who 18 as good ns any other 
in the Colony, and if partnered by 


. Rowlands

sadly: missed, .

AN, OLD FAVOURITE 


Amongst those who: watched the 
trial was Talbot, the Royal Welch’: 
Fusiliers’ inside forward, who re- 
turned to the Cotony with his batta- 
lion from Shanghal on Saturday. 
He will be Icaving this week-end, 
but few know that with a little better 
Juck we might have had him with us 
as he was desirous of joining the 
Police Force. This ts not to be, 
however, and the Colony thus loses 
another flne footballer. .

MACAO 
HELD TO. 
A DRAW 


C.B.A. Pays Visit . 
During Week-end 


Macao, Feb. 6. 

A lively hockey match was played 
here this afternoon between the 
teams of the Central British Assocla- 
tlon and ‘the Macao Hockey Club, 
resulting in a draw of 1-1, 4 

Both teams experlenced consider- 
able difficulty owing to the slippiness 
of the ground. but the visitors ex- 
hibited n splendid combination both 
In attack and defence, Maco play- 
ed vigorously throughout. In_ the 
nbsence of Alex Alrosa, centre-half, 
Laertes Costa took this position for 
the local side, while Mario’ Soares 
‘took over the left-hal{ berth. 

From the outset, play was fast 
and only seven minutes from bully- 
off, Ramalho, centre-forward, sccured 
a splendid goat following a smart 
breakaway. 

The visitors equalised three minutes 
later when a tricky shot by Fowler, 
centre-forward, found the net, Al- 
mada, the Macao goal-keeper, was 
out of his position. 

Several mids followed and it was 
touch-and-go as to which side would 
win even up to the lust minute. The 
forwards of both sides were unlucky. 
The mediocre standard of referecing 
gave rise to much criticism. 

Teams: 

Centra! Britlsh—J. J. 
Taylor, V. Bond; E. Fowler, N. 
Whitley, D. Blake; D, Smith, S. 
Fowler, G..Fowler, T. Whitley and 
S. MeNider, 

Macao, — Almada; > Lammert,  R. 
Rosario; Ferreira, lL. Costu, M. 
Soares; F. Notasco, Albert Alrosa, 
Ramulho, H. Rosario and Angelo.—

REVOLTA WINS 
GOLF TOURNEY 


Sacramento, Cal, Feb. 6. 
In three days of driving rain, 
Johnny Revolta; the American Ryder 
Cup golfer, won the $3,000 Open 
Tournament here with n score of 

290, which is 22 strokes over par, 
Ghezzl was second with 201, ond 
Ben Hogan, of Fort Worth, Texas, 
third with 202,—United Press, :

Davos, Switz, Feb. 6.

TENNIS SERIES

Each of these signs js given o 
symbol, so to read an __ astrological 
chart requircs a Knowledge of astro- 
logical shorthand, -Ench of _ the 
planets is also given a symbol, Each 
positional relation sikewise. 

The Involved rules of casting a 
horoscope require n book to explain 
them. -The Signs ure grouped Into 
triplicitles—groups of three—each 
representing one of the primitive 
elements—earth, air, fire, water. 
Each member of ench triplicity is 
regurded as a sub-ruler of a certain 
ten duys of the month controlled by 
the main sign. 

The Signs are again grouped into 
quadriplicities—groups of four. 
They are characterised as movable, 
fixed and mutable. 

The positions and relations of the 
stars now regulate the character— 
say the Astrologists. ' The Sign can 
be House, Detriment. Exaltation: or 
Fall. Thot means, it can occupy a 
position within its’ own House: 
opposite to ‘those it rules (when it 
is evil); on the righthand, or on the 
ett, 


Nov. 22—Dec. 22 


Then, planets—Mercury, Venus, 
Mars, Jupiter, Saturn, Uranus, Nep- 
tunc—and the Sun und Moon are 
“aspected” to the Signs. They can 
be parallel, in. conjunction, 30 de- 
grees away (semiesextile); 45 de- 
urecs away (semiesquare): 60 de- 


Rreey away (sextile); 
away (square); 120 degrees away 
(trinc); 135 degrees away. (sesqui- 
quadrate); 150 degrees away (quin- 
cunx) or in opposition; 180 degrees 
(a whole semi-circle) away. 
get them further away thon a half- 
circle, you measure on the other 
half-clrele. 

The square is bad, the’ trine 
good; conjunctions and parallels. 
ure variable; sextiles and semi- 
sextiles are yoodish; the others bad 
to very bad, 

Trine is good, but three plancts 
in trine is bad, The nearmess or re- 
moteness of planets from exact 
positions controls the degree of in- 
tluence the .aspect bears: so that If 
u planet is very nearly In trine, it 
is pretty good; if far away, much 
less good. . 

Measurements are now taken first 
from the Sun to the Moon, then 
from the Sun to ench planct in turn. 
The next serics of measurements Is 
from the Moon to the planets in 
turn. Then from Mercury to the 
other planets; then from Venus, 
and so on untll all relative positions 
have been calculated, 

Further calculations reduce astral 
time to Greenwich time or B.E.T, 
Now the characteristics of 
planets are taken Into account: -

masculine, strong, sanguine. 

Moon: Cold, moist, phicymatic, 

negative, feminine, changeable, 

Mercury: Cold, nervous, change- 

‘ able, excitable, bi-sexual, busy. 

Venus (Lesser Fortune): Magnetic,

policy. 

“We cnnnot say the German cactlon 
will go to extremes or even ciiusce

German intervention in Spain will be

will_draw closer together. 

“General von Blomberg was for 
many years ‘sympathetic to China. 
We regret his removal. China waits 
and hopes that Germany’s policy 
won't turn out to be fayouruble. to 
Japan.”

ation and suggests the Chinese ought 
to imltate such swift determination.

years ,younger than those retired, 
thereby hinting at the capabilities of: 
junior oflicers.

Cordell Hull Appeals 
To “Hermit’’ Nations 


: Washington, Feb, 6. 

The willingness of the United 
States Government to limit or 
reduce armaments was ex- 


pressed by Mr, Cordell Hull, the y 


Secretary of State, in a broad- 
cast over a nation-wide network 
to-day, : 


the European |:

HIGHEST. 
FOR YEAR 


IMPROVEMENT SOON 


During the 24 hours ending 
10 a. m. to-day humidity: reach- 
ed 95 per cent., the highest re- 
corded in the Colony since last 
summer, and only five per cent. 
below absolute saturation. 

Variation between minimum and 
maximum temperature during- the 
24 houra was only twe'degrees—from 
66 to 58 degrees. 

“A depression Is situated to the cast 
of Hokkaido, and‘ an anti-cyclone 
covers China and the nelghbouring 
‘seas, extending eastward to the 
Bonlns, .

(may improve shortly, although. fur- 
ther cloudy conditions with rain are 
forecast.

cloudy with occastonal rain, probably

““While, in a world in which in- 


creased construction of armaments fs 
u regreltable fact, we are compelled 
to render adequate our military and 
naval cstablishments, we are ready 
at any time to join other nations ina 
common cffort to bring about a 
general limitatlon or reduction of 


-armaments,”-Mr-Hull- declared: 

The Seeretary of State attacked 
what he termed “hermit nations,” 
meaning nations which were secking 
economic self-sufliciency. 

He declared that economic warfare 
was largely responsible for the re- 
cent alarming disintegration of inter- 
national relationships, and war, and 
the fear of war, were gripping the 
minds and stultifying the spirit of 
mankind. 

Feverlsh preparatlons for potential 
armed conflicts were sapping 
already badly Impaired cconomic 
health of many nations. 

“The world desperately necds In- 
ternational order bascd on the re- 
vitalisntion of international jaw," Mr, 
Hull declared,

offering ay one means of peuce his

. Honkow, Feb. 7.- 

The | American. Embassy ‘ond 
missionaries here have not heard 
onythin: of tho report of the. 
kidnapping of Miss Helen Boughton, 
of the Northern Presbyterian’ Mission 
at Hulayunon, close to which fighting 
between Chinese and Japanese troops 
ig raging at present. . 

This mission ‘included six women. 
nnd one man, Miss’ Boughton, - of 
Trenton, NwJ., one of them. The 
others at the nilssion are believed to 
be Miss Mabel Hall, New York, Miss 
Elisabeth Turner, of St, Alban’‘s, 
West Virginia, Miss Harriet Stroh, of 
Wheaton, Hlinois, Miss Hattie Mac-" 
Curdy, of Madison, NJ. who has 
Canndlon relatives, and Miss Mirlam 
Petchner, whose home address is not 
known. Dr. R. J. MacCandiiss, a 
Chinu-born mission doctor, is the 
only man at the mission, 

Mr. David B. van Dyke and his 
wife are at present belicved to be 
attempting to reach the station at 
Hunaiyuan. 

A letter dated January 31, from 
Hunalyuan, stated that the place wns 
quiet, with few Chinese soldiers 
hbout. There hod been many visits 
from nircraft but no bombings—.

Saleswoman_ 
With £10,000 
Expenses. Is 
Cone 


. London, Jan. 30. 
A saleswoman with £10,000 for 
expenses will shorily arrive in 
Hongkong. 


She is Mrs. ‘Eugente Daubeny, who 
leaves England next week to act; in 
her own words, as “contact woman 
between British “manufacturers ond 


{buyers In the Colonies and Doml- 


nions,.” 


She is going’ to “link up with ‘the 
peopic on.the other. side,- discover 
their needs, and then tell.them all 
about British firms which can supply 
them.” 


“My ambition," Mrs. Daubeny 
adds, “ig to open up channels for 
millions of pounds’ worth of trade 
in goods of every concelvable des- 
cription.. I am not taking any sam- 
ples, because I am not a salesman 
in that sense, but I have six trunks 
full of catalogues.” 

Mrs. Daubeny claims that -her Igst 
tour yielded more than £250,000 of 
business and declares that she will 
not be satisfied with less than several 
milllon pounds worth this. time.
